“But, the issue itself is so… Persistent. Unpredictable. Unmanageable.”

Husk frowned. “Something happen?”

Alastor stopped walking. His breath hitched. His grip on his cane tightened.

Charlie waited, patient and calm.

After several long seconds, Alastor spoke in a low voice, the words edged with shame and dread. “…I have been having these… Dreams.”

Husk blinked. “Dreams?”

Alastor nodded once, stiffly. “Dreams in which Vox discovers my condition.”

Charlie’s expression softened immediately. “Oh, Alastor…”

He pressed his lips together, eyes narrowing as if recalling the images. “I see him laughing,” Alastor whispered, voice trembling again. “Broadcasting it to the masses. Mocking me. Turning something private— Humiliating— Into a spectacle.” Static crackled faintly at his shoulders, a clear sign of distress. “And then… I wake. And the anxiety lingers. And the next time I need to—” His voice faltered and he cleared his throat. “—My body remembers the dream instead of the reality.”

Charlie stepped gently in front of him, touching his arm lightly with permission in her eyes. “Alastor,” she said softly, “Dreams aren’t real. But the fear they cause? That’s real. And it can make everything harder.”

He looked away, jaw tightening. “It is… Humiliating. How profoundly it affects me.”

Husk shrugged. “Fear’s fear, man. Doesn’t matter if you’re the Radio Demon or a guy sweeping floors.”

Alastor huffed weakly. “Perhaps.”

Charlie shook her head gently, her voice warm but steady. “Vox isn’t here. He doesn’t know. And we won’t let him know. You’re safe with us. Truly.”

Alastor’s eyes flicked toward hers— Guarded at first, then slowly softening.

“And we’ll help you,” Charlie continued. “With the dreams, and the anxiety, and the grounding exercises. You don’t have to fight this alone.”

He swallowed hard. The static quieted. His posture eased—Not fully, but measurably.

“Your promise is…” Alastor searched for the word. His voice softened— Raw, small, real. “Reassuring.”

Charlie smiled, squeezing his arm lightly before releasing him.

Husk nudged him with his wing. “Hey. We got your back. Even when your bladder doesn’t.”

“Husk,” Charlie hissed.

But Alastor surprised them both; He laughed. Barely more than a breath—but a genuine, relieved laugh. “…I appreciate the sentiment,” he said.

They continued walking, slowly but together, the worst behind them, the weight no longer on Alastor alone.

*006*2026-04-13 12:22

The idea started, as most of Charlie’s ideas did, with optimism so bright it bordered on recklessness.

“Okay, hear me out,” she said, hands clasped tightly in front of her chest, eyes shining as she stood in the lobby of the Hazbin Hotel. “What if we all just… Went on a trip?”

A beat of silence followed.

Alastor’s smile didn’t flicker, but his radio-static chuckle crackled through the air. “A trip, my dear princess? Where, exactly?”

Vaggie crossed her arms, already suspicious. “Charlie, last time you said that, we ended up mediating a turf dispute that ended with half the street on fire.”

“That was ONE time,” Charlie protested. “And it was mostly on fire BEFORE we got there.”

Angel Dust, draped upside-down over the back of a couch, lifted his head. “Define ‘trip.’ Because I didn’t pack heels for a wilderness survival arc.”

Charlie inhaled deeply, as if bracing herself against all of Hell’s collective cynicism, then beamed even wider. “A drive! Just a long drive. No agenda. No redemption seminars. No violence—” she shot a pointed look at Angel and Alastor “—And no drinking and driving,” she added, glancing at Husk.

Husk raised both hands. “Then I ain’t drivin’ at all.”

Niffty popped up from nowhere, already vibrating with excitement. “Ooo! A road trip! Do we clean the car first? I can clean the car! I love cleaning cars!”

Vaggie sighed, rubbing her temples. “Charlie… You don’t have a car.”

Charlie’s smile faltered for exactly half a second. “Well,” she said carefully, “We can use my dad’s.”

Alastor’s grin widened. “Ahhh. Now this does sound promising.”

Lucifer’s car was less a vehicle and more a statement.

It sat like a polished obsidian beast, long and low, with chrome accents that gleamed like fangs under the red Hell-sky. The engine purred even when it was off, humming with restrained power and something faintly infernal. The interior was upholstered in deep crimson leather, stitched with gold sigils that glowed faintly when touched. The hood, naturally, had a massive duck painted on it.

Charlie stood beside it, bouncing slightly on her heels.

“Okay! Seating arrangement!” she announced.

Vaggie immediately took the front passenger seat, because if this was happening, she was not letting Charlie drive alone. Charlie herself took the wheel, fingers brushing the steering wheel with reverent excitement.

Angel sprawled dramatically across the backseat, legs stretched out. Husk followed, muttering about legroom and damnation, and Niffty somehow wedged herself between them, already wiping down a spotless window with a rag she produced from nowhere.

That left Alastor.

He paused at the open door, tilting his head as he surveyed the interior. “How delightfully intimate,” he said, before smoothly sliding into the remaining seat, folding himself in with eerie grace. “I do adore confined spaces. So much potential.”

Vaggie shot him a glare. “Don’t make this weird.”

“Oh, my dear,” Alastor replied pleasantly. “I never make anything weird. I merely observe.”

Charlie turned the key. The engine roared to life, deep and resonant, and the car eased forwards, rolling out onto one of Hell’s many winding highways— This one stretching long and empty, neon signs flickering lazily in the distance.

For a while, no one spoke.

Hell blurred past the windows: Jagged skylines, flickering billboards advertising sins in bold, garish colors, distant fires glowing like fallen stars. The road stretched on, smoother than expected, humming softly beneath the tires.

Charlie exhaled, shoulders relaxing as she settled into the rhythm of driving.

“See?” she said softly. “Isn’t this… Kind of nice?”

Angel stared out the window, unusually quiet. “Huh. Didn’t think I’d say this, but… Yeah. Kinda is.”

Husk took another swig from his bottle, then paused, frowning. “Road’s too quiet.”

“That’s because it’s a secondary route,” Charlie explained eagerly. “Dad used to take me on these when I was little. Said they were good for thinking.”

Vaggie glanced at her. “Thinking about what?”

Charlie smiled, eyes on the road ahead. “About where you’re going… And who you’re going with.”

The car continued on, cutting a steady path through Hell’s endless night, carrying with it a strange, fragile peace— One that none of them quite trusted, but none of them were ready to break.

Neon gave way to dimmer lights. Towers thinned into low, skeletal buildings. Eventually, even the billboards disappeared, replaced by long expanses of cracked asphalt and scorched plains that shimmered faintly under the red sky.

Vaggie tried to focus on Charlie and not the ridiculous argument Angel had engaged Alastor in.

“Not my Maman,” Alastor was saying.

“Uh, Al?” Angel said. “I dunno how to tell ya this, but your mom DEFINITELY had sex at least once.”

“Preposterous, Angel!” Alastor said. “You lack even the tiniest shred of evidence for this ludicrous claim!”

Angel leaned back. “…Smiles, I can’t believe I’m asking ya this, but do you know where babies come from?”

“Of course I do,” Alastor said. “I’d just prefer not to describe it.”

“Okay,” Angel nodded. “Soooo… That would mean…”

Alastor tilted his head, raising one ear. “Would mean what?”

“That your mom did it at LEAST once.”

“I still see no proof.”

“Alastor, dude, you ARE the—“

“Angel,” Husk interrupted. “That’s enough. You ain’t gonna make him accept it.”

Angel shrugged, then hummed along to the radio Charlie had tuned in, tapping claws against his knee. Niffty had long since curled up, asleep against the window, her head leaving a tiny foggy patch on the glass. Husk stared straight ahead, bottle resting loosely in his hand, eyes half-lidded but alert.

Vaggie kept sneaking glances at Charlie, who looked… Lighter. Like the simple act of moving forward without a crisis to fix had loosened something tight inside her.

And, after a while, Alastor sat very still.

Trying his best to ignore a faint pressure. He was very good at ignoring things— Pain, discomfort, the gnawing echoes of things best left unexamined. He crossed one leg over the other, posture immaculate, smile unchanged.

Minutes passed. Then more.

The pressure returned, sharper now, insistent in a way that tugged at the edge of his awareness.

‘Not now,’ he thought, adjusting his grip on his cane. He shifted slightly in his seat. No one noticed. The road hummed on.

Charlie chatted about old roadside attractions she remembered. Vaggie responded with soft murmurs and the occasional smile. Angel cracked jokes that didn’t quite land, then shrugged and leaned back again.

Alastor listened, nodding at appropriate intervals, offering the occasional polite chuckle. Outwardly, he was the picture of ease.

Inwardly, he was doing mental arithmetic.

‘We have been driving for… How long, exactly?’

He glanced out the window. No signs. No buildings. Certainly no private rest stops. The landscape had grown barren, the road slicing through it like a scar.

He adjusted again, uncrossing and recrossing his legs with careful precision.

Still, he said nothing.

To announce such a need— To interrupt this fragile little tableau with something so… Base— Was unthinkable. The Radio Demon did not whine. He did not fidget. He did not ask for bathroom breaks like a misbehaving child on a school trip.

Absolutely not.

Another stretch of road passed.

The pressure grew harder to dismiss now, a dull ache that demanded attention. His smile tightened, just a fraction, though the static in his voice remained perfectly even when he spoke.

“My, my,” he said conversationally, “this drive HAS been quite extensive, hasn’t it?”

Charlie glanced at the dashboard clock. “Huh. Yeah! Guess we’ve been out for a while.”

Vaggie frowned slightly. “You okay, Alastor?”

“Splendid,” he replied instantly.

Angel squinted at him from the other side of the seat. “You’re bein’ weird.”

Alastor turned his head slowly, grin sharp. “And you’re only just now noticing?”

Angel snorted and let it drop.

The road dipped, then rose again, stretching on like it had no intention of ending. Alastor’s fingers tightened around his cane, knuckles whitening beneath his gloves. He kept his posture rigid, movements minimal, every shift calculated to maintain control.

‘Hold it,’ he told himself, with the same iron certainty he applied to everything else. ‘This is nothing.’

Another long while passed, and the ache sharpened into something urgent. Alastor’s knee bounced once before he forced it still. His eyes flicked towards the windows, the empty land beyond them offering no solutions whatsoever.

Asking Charlie when the next stop was would mean explaining why. Explaining would mean vulnerability. And vulnerability was unacceptable.

Up front, Charlie slowed slightly, peering ahead. “That’s weird,” she murmured.

Vaggie leaned forward. “What?”

“I thought there’d be a stop by now,” Charlie said.

Alastor straightened, attention snapping forward despite himself. “Oh?” he said lightly, though his voice carried the faintest edge. “Do you intend to stop soon, my dear?”

Charlie hesitated. “I mean… I CAN, if someone needs to.”

Silence settled in the car.

Alastor stared ahead, smile frozen, pride warring fiercely with biology. “…I don’t,” he said at last, far too smoothly. “Merely curious,” he explained, teeth clenched behind his ever-present grin.

The drive went on.

Not the peaceful, drifting-on-momentum kind anymore, but the sort where time thickened, stretching each minute until it felt deliberate, cruel, even. The car’s engine maintained its steady purr, tires whispering against asphalt, an unbroken rhythm that mocked Alastor’s increasingly fractured concentration.

He no longer bothered pretending to himself that he was comfortable.

Every muscle in his lower body was drawn tight with restraint, posture held together by sheer force of will. He sat ramrod-straight, shoulders squared, chin lifted as if daring discomfort itself to challenge him. His legs were pressed together now, an uncharacteristically rigid pose that he prayed no one would notice.

They noticed other things.

“Man,” Angel muttered after a while, stretching his arms over his head, “How long we been drivin’? Feels like forever.”

“Almost three hours,” Vaggie said, checking the clock.

Charlie winced. “Oh! I didn’t realize it’d been that long. I guess I just… Kept going.”

She glanced in the rearview mirror. “Everyone okay back there?”

“Fine,” Husk grunted.

Angel gave a lazy thumbs-up. “Still alive. Emotionally questionable, but alive.”

Niffty snored softly, blissfully unaware of the concept of time, space, or Alastor’s bladder capacity.

Charlie’s eyes flicked, just briefly, to Alastor.

He met her gaze instantly, smile flawless, eyes sharp and bright. “Quite fine,” he said, voice smooth as lacquer. “Please, do continue.”

The pressure surged again, harsher this time, and he very nearly inhaled sharply before catching himself. His fingers curled into his cane so tightly the wood creaked in protest.

‘This is absurd,’ he thought, a flicker of irritation cutting through his usual composure. ‘I can hold it. I don’t need to ask for…’

He stopped that line of thought immediately.

No. Absolutely not.

He shifted his weight a fraction of an inch, then froze when the movement sent a spike of urgency through him. His grin twitched, just barely, static crackling faintly at its edges before he forced it back into place.

Angel, unfortunately, was observant in the most inconvenient way. “Yo,” he said, tilting his head. “You alright, Radiohead? You’re, like… Statuesque today.”

Alastor turned to him slowly. “Must you persist in addressing me with such dreadful nicknames?”

Angel smirked. “Only when you look like you’re about to snap someone in half.”

“Rest assured,” Alastor replied, voice just a shade tighter than usual, “If I were on the verge of snapping, you would be the first to know.”

Angel studied him for another second, then shrugged. “Whatever you say, man.”

The road dipped again, the car swaying gently as it followed the curve. Alastor’s breath hitched despite himself. He squeezed his eyes shut for half a heartbeat, then reopened them, staring straight ahead like the landscape had personally offended him.

Up front, Charlie slowed slightly. “Okay,” she said thoughtfully. “Maybe I should pull over soon. Just in case.”

Alastor’s heart— Black, shriveled thing though it was— Lurched.

No. No, no, no.

If she stopped here, that would do him no good. There was no privacy whatsoever along the side of the road. He wouldn’t be able to let go. It would only further delay his return to the hotel, to his room, to relief…

“There’s truly no need to trouble yourself,” he said quickly. “I’m certain we can manage a while longer.”

Vaggie frowned. “Alastor, are you sure?”

“Entirely.” The word came out clipped, precise, final.

Charlie hesitated, then nodded. “Okay. If you say so.”

The car picked up speed again.

Alastor swallowed hard, jaw tight. The ache had evolved into something desperate now, a constant, screaming demand that drowned out even the hum of the engine. He could feel it in every breath, every tiny vibration of the road.

He counted things to distract himself. The white lines passing beneath the car. The rhythm of the tires. The static whisper of his own power.

‘Just a little longer, surely,’ he told himself. ‘You have endured worse.’

But this wasn’t something he could intimidate or outmaneuver. It was a betrayal from within, a weakness he had no elegant solution for.

Another involuntary movement betrayed him— A subtle tightening, a shift he couldn’t entirely suppress.

Vaggie noticed. She leaned closer to Charlie, whispering, “Is it just me, or is Alastor… REALLY tense?”

Charlie glanced back again, concern knitting her brow. “Alastor?”

He straightened even further, if that was possible. “Yes?”

“Are you okay?”

For one terrifying moment, the words nearly slipped out. The truth hovered on his tongue, hot and humiliating, and his smile wavered—Just a hairline crack. ‘As a matter of fact, no. I need to relieve myself most urgently, and it seems you’ve found a region that is devoid of even the smallest sliver of privacy!’

Then pride slammed down like a guillotine.

“I assure you,” he said, voice carefully measured, “I am perfectly fine.”

Charlie’s shoulders drooped slightly, but she nodded. “Okay.”

The car rolled on.

With every mile that slipped by, Vaggie’s gaze kept drifting to the rearview mirror, landing again and again on one very stiff, very silent Radio Demon.

‘Something is wrong with him,’ she thought.

Alastor wasn’t fidgety by nature, but he also wasn’t… Whatever THIS was. His posture was too rigid, his smile too fixed, like a porcelain mask glued in place. He hadn’t made a single obnoxious comment in over ten minutes, which frankly bordered on apocalyptic.

She leaned a little closer to Charlie and lowered her voice. “Hey. Slow down a sec.”

Charlie eased off the accelerator. “What’s up?”

Vaggie nodded subtly toward the back seat. “It’s Alastor.”

Charlie glanced in the mirror again, worry flaring instantly. “Oh no— Do you think he’s upset? Did I do something?”

“No,” Vaggie said quickly. “He’s not angry. He looks…” She searched for the word. “…Off.”

Charlie squinted. “You think he’s sick?”

“Carsick,” Vaggie said with sudden certainty. “Think about it. Long drive. No stops. He’s barely moving, staring straight ahead, trying not to react.”

Angel perked up immediately. “Oh, shit. Is the scary radio man gonna puke?”

Alastor’s head snapped towards them. “I beg your pardon?”

Vaggie twisted around in her seat, addressing him directly now, “Alastor. Are you feeling nauseous?”

The question hit him like a brick. Nauseous? He hesitated for half a beat too long, then smoothed his expression. “I fail to see how my internal state is any of your concern.”

Angel grinned. “That’s a yes.”

“It is not,” Alastor snapped, then immediately reined himself in. “I am merely… Unaccustomed to prolonged vehicular confinement.”

Vaggie exchanged a look with Charlie that said ‘called it.’

“Okay,” Charlie said gently, already reaching for the turn signal. “That’s totally fine! We can stop. There’s no rush.”

Alastor’s pulse spiked.

Stopping meant prolonging this misery.

“No!” he said sharply.

Everyone froze.

Even the car seemed to hesitate.

Alastor cleared his throat, static flaring faintly before settling. “I mean— There is no need to make a fuss. It will pass.”

Angel blinked. “Dude, if you’re gonna hurl, I would very much like it NOT to be in my lap.”

“I am not going to do that,” Alastor said through clenched teeth.

Husk glanced over, unimpressed. “You look like you’re about to pass out or barf. Either way, that’s a mess.”

Charlie slowed the car anyway. “Alastor, it’s okay. Carsickness happens. We won’t judge.”

Vaggie nodded. “Yeah. Seriously. I get it too, sometimes.”

Alastor stared straight ahead, every nerve screaming. The road sign ahead flickered into view— Some forgotten turnout, cracked pavement leading off into emptiness and nothing even resembling seclusion.

“I said I’m fine,” he repeated, quieter now, but iron-hard. “Please. Continue driving.”

Charlie hesitated, torn. “Vaggie…?”

Vaggie frowned, eyes narrowing. She studied Alastor again— Really studied him this time. The tension in his shoulders. The way his hands trembled, just barely, around his cane. The way his legs were pressed together like he was bracing against something invisible.

She’d prefer not to see what Alastor’s typical meals looked like when regurgitated.

“…Charlie,” she said slowly, “Pull over.”

Alastor’s breath hitched.

“What?” Charlie asked.

“Just do it,” Vaggie said, tone leaving no room for argument. “I’m NOT gonna be stuck scraping Alastor’s vomit out of your dad’s car.”

Niffty opened her eye for a second. “Oooh, can I do that?!”

“Niffty, I’m fine,” Alastor sighed.

The car eased off the road, gravel crunching beneath the tires as it slowed.

Alastor’s smile finally cracked— Not enough for anyone else to see it, but enough for him to feel it shatter.

Trapped. Exposed. Desperate.

Silence followed— Thick, expectant, broken only by the ticking of the cooling engine and the distant, hollow wind scraping across the empty plains. Charlie put the car in park and twisted around in her seat, concern written openly across her face.

“Okay,” she said gently. “We’re stopped. Nobody’s in trouble. Let’s just… Take a breather.”

This area was worse than Alastor had feared.

Flat. Open. Exposed.

The road stretched endlessly in both directions, a thin black ribbon cutting through a barren expanse of cracked earth and dead brush. No buildings. No rocks large enough to shield anything. Not even a crooked signpost. Just empty land beneath a red sky that felt far too wide and far too watchful.

Alastor took one look and knew he could not urinate here.

So, he didn’t move.

Angel hopped out first, stretching. “Wow. Middle of nowhere, huh? Real murder-documentary vibes.”

Husk followed more slowly, lighting up a cigarette and scanning the horizon. “Yeah. Ain’t even a place to piss without the universe starin’ back at ya.”

The words hit Alastor like a cruel punchline… As did the sound of Husk’s stream a moment later, as the cat demon apparently decided that the lack of privacy wasn’t a problem for him.

Charlie glanced back. “Alastor? You can step out if you want. Fresh air might help.”

He remained seated, hands folded neatly over the head of his cane, legs still pressed tightly together. His smile was back in place—Perfect, polished, and utterly brittle. “How considerate,” he said. “But I see no reason to disembark.”

Vaggie frowned, stepping out of the car and then turning back toward him. “You’ve been locked up in here for hours. You sure you don’t need a minute?”

Alastor’s gaze flicked past her, over the empty landscape.

A minute out there would be a minute on display.

“No,” he said simply.

Charlie hesitated, clearly trying to reconcile what she was seeing with what she was hearing. “If it’s about… Being sick, we can all turn around.”

Angel snorted. “Yeah, we’ll give you your puking privacy, princess.”

Alastor’s smile sharpened, just a touch too tight. “Your concern is touching. Truly. But I assure you, I am not ill. I am perfectly well.”

That was a lie, of course. The truth was simple and brutal: There was nowhere here he could go without being seen, and being seen was worse than the discomfort. Worse than the strain in his body, worse than the relentless urgency clawing at his composure.

So, he stayed put.

Minutes dragged by.

Angel wandered a little distance away, talking animatedly at Husk about something. Niffty hopped out, spun in a circle, and immediately started picking up trash. Charlie leaned against the car, arms folded, chewing her lip.

Vaggie, however, didn’t leave.

She stood by the open back door, watching Alastor with a sharp, worried intensity. “You’re really not okay,” she said quietly, so only he could hear.

He met her eyes, grin unwavering. “I am exceptionally okay.”

She didn’t buy it for a second. “You don’t have to explain,” she said. “But if you need something, say it. We’re not gonna mock you.”

Alastor’s fingers twitched once against his cane, betraying him. ‘PERHAPS, if you simply SAY that you need to relieve yourself, Charlie will find you some privacy. Of course she stopped HERE when she doesn’t know what you actually need…’

He could see it clearly, Charlie exclaiming; “Oh no, Alastor! THAT’S what’s wrong? I’m sorry! This is a terrible spot for that! I’ll find you a better one right away!”

And then, he’d have relief in just a short while. Desperately needed relief…

He didn’t admit it, of course.

“I appreciate your concern,” he said coolly. “But I am fine. Now, unless you intend to linger here indefinitely, I suggest we resume our journey.”

Vaggie searched his face, clearly fighting the urge to push harder. Finally, she exhaled through her nose and straightened. “…Fine,” she said.

She closed the door.

Charlie looked between them, confused but obedient, and slid back into the driver’s seat. One by one, the others piled back in, the car settling under their weight.

The engine turned over again.

As the car pulled back onto the road and the empty area disappeared behind them, Alastor allowed himself exactly one shallow, shaky breath.

He had made his choice. There would be no relief. Not now. Not here. Only endurance.

The road swallowed them again.

Mile after mile slid past, each indistinguishable from the last, dark asphalt, faint lane markings, a horizon that never seemed to draw any closer. The car’s interior felt smaller now, tighter, as if the space itself were conspiring against him.

Alastor no longer pretended that this was tolerable.

Every bump in the road sent a sharp jolt through him, each vibration a reminder of how close he was to bursting. His breathing had gone shallow, measured, carefully rationed. He stared straight ahead, eyes fixed and unblinking, smile locked in place like a punishment he’d inflicted on himself.

‘Hold it. Hold it. Hold it.’

That was all there was anymore.

He couldn’t shift his position without consequence, couldn’t relax even for a second. His entire body was drawn in, coiled tight, the effort of restraint eclipsing thought itself. The humiliation of admitting this still felt worse than the pain, so he stayed silent.

Up front, Charlie chatted half-heartedly now, her earlier buoyancy dulled by the growing tension in the car. Vaggie remained alert, glancing back far more often than she liked, clearly unsatisfied with Alastor’s insistence that nothing was wrong.

In the back seat, Angel Dust shifted once. Then again. Then, he crossed his legs, uncrossed them, drummed his fingers against his knee.

“Okay,” he muttered at last, breaking the quiet. “Not to be that guy, but… How much longer we drivin’?”

Charlie checked the road, then the clock. “Uh… I’m not totally sure. There should be a stop eventually.”

Angel groaned. “Fantastic.”

Husk side-eyed him. “What’s your problem now?”

Angel shot him a look. “Ah, nothin’ Just should’ve pissed while we were pulled over, fuzzball.”

Husk huffed. “You mean like I told you to?”

Angel leaned back, then immediately leaned forward again, grimacing. “I swear, if we don’t stop soon, I’m gonna explode.”

Charlie glanced back, startled. “Oh! Angel, why didn’t you go before?”

Angel threw his hands up. “Because I didn’t have to go then!”

Vaggie exhaled sharply. “Charlie, we need to find a place to pull over. Soon.”

Charlie nodded, immediately slowing. “Okay. Okay. I’ll keep an eye out.”

Alastor’s heart pounded painfully in his chest. This was it. This was his chance. Angel had said it— Openly, shamelessly. The excuse was right there. He could follow. He could step out under the same justification, blend into the group, preserve some fraction of dignity.

All he had to do was agree.

The words rose up, burning and desperate, and died behind his teeth.

He couldn’t.

He wouldn’t.

Alastor stared ahead, fingers trembling against his cane as another surge of urgency ripped through him, sharp enough to steal his breath. His smile wavered, static hissing faintly at its edges before he forced it still.

Charlie scanned the road anxiously. “There’s—There’s nothing out here. I don’t see any cover.”

Angel groaned. “Great. Just great.”

The car continued forward, the tension inside it thick enough to choke on.

Alastor sat rigid, every nerve screaming, every instinct demanding release, while he kept silent.

Beside him, Angel bounced his knee, muttering under his breath.

The car did not stop.

It couldn’t, apparently.

Charlie slowed whenever the road widened, craned her neck whenever a shadow suggested cover, but every promising turn dissolved into more emptiness. Flat land. No structures. No breaks in the terrain. Hell, in all its irony, had decided to be wide open and mercilessly public.

Angel, meanwhile, had lost all subtlety. “Oh my God,” he groaned, clutching the back of the front seat. “I am NOT built for endurance challenges like this.”

Husk didn’t even look at him. “You’ll live.”

“I will not,” Angel snapped. “I will die. Right here. In this car. And it will be everyone’s fault.”

Charlie winced sympathetically. “I’m really trying, Angel. I swear.”

“Try faster,” Angel said. He turned to one side, then immediately sucked in a sharp breath and crossed his legs tight. “Ohhh— Nope. Bad idea. Very bad idea.”

Vaggie glanced back again, jaw tight. “Angel, be quiet.”

“I am narrating my suffering,” Angel shot back. “It’s called honesty.”

Alastor said nothing. He could barely hear them anymore. The world had narrowed to sensation— Hot, relentless, unbearable. His entire lower body felt like a drawn bowstring, pulled far past what it was meant to endure. Each second stretched thin, threatening to snap. His breath came in shallow increments, carefully controlled, like even inhaling too deeply might undo him.

His legs were pressed together so tightly it bordered on painful. His hands— Normally so steady— Had begun to tremble outright, fingers clenched white-knuckled around his cane as though it were the only thing anchoring him to reality.

His smile was still there, but it was wrong. Too stiff. Too sharp. Like a painted expression cracking on old porcelain.

Another jolt of the road sent a spike of agony through him, and he hissed softly through his teeth before he could stop himself. Static flared faintly at the edges of his form, like his power itself was reacting to the strain.

Charlie glanced back sharply. “Alastor—?”

He lifted a gloved hand, palm outward. A clear, unmistakable ‘don’t.’

She froze.

Angel turned back toward the windshield, voice rising again as the pressure mounted for him too. “Charlie, I am beggin’ you. I don’t care if it’s behind a rock, a sign, or a cursed tumbleweed. I need a place. NOW.”

“I’m looking!” Charlie said, panic creeping into her voice.

The road stretched on, uncaring.

Alastor felt like he might burst— Like his body was an explosive he could no longer control. Every instinct screamed at him to speak, to plead, to do something.

And yet— He sat there. Silent. Rigid. Smiling.

Angel groaned loudly, rocking forward in his seat. “This is actual torture.”

Alastor didn’t disagree. But even now, when he was desperate, shaking, and pushed far beyond his limits… His shyness, his pride, that deep, ingrained refusal to admit need, kept his mouth shut.

The car sped onward through the empty expanse of Hell, carrying two demons rapidly approaching their breaking points, and only one of them willing to say it out loud.

If Hell had wanted to design the perfect torture for Alastor, it could not have done better.

The pressure had gone past discomfort, past urgency, into something sharp and overwhelming— An all-consuming demand that drowned out thought. His vision swam at the edges, the world narrowing until there was only the road ahead and the iron grip of control he was forcing on himself.

He had never felt so close to losing.

His legs were locked together now, posture utterly rigid, shoulders drawn tight as wire. Every muscle trembled with strain. His breathing had become a careful, shallow thing, measured like he was defusing a bomb inside his own body.

‘Any second,’ his mind whispered. ‘Any second now.’

Beside him, Angel Dust had fully abandoned dignity. “Oh my GOD,” he groaned, doubling forward and clutching his knee. “I am actually going to explode. This ain’t a joke anymore.”

Husk snorted. “You’ve been sayin’ that for twenty minutes.”

“Yeah, and it’s been TRUE for twenty minutes!” Angel snapped. “Charlie, I swear, if I don’t get to a bathroom soon, I’m—MMF—!” He cut himself off with a sharp inhale, crossing his legs tightly. “Nope. Nope. That was a close one.”

Vaggie shot him an exasperated look. “Angel!”

“What? Some of us process our trauma verbally.”

Alastor said nothing. He couldn’t trust himself to speak. Not now. Not when even opening his mouth felt dangerous, like it might crack the fragile restraint holding him together, might cause him to blurt out something like “MY BACK TEETH ARE FLOATING.”

Charlie gripped the steering wheel, eyes darting back and forth desperately. “I’m so sorry. I didn’t think it’d be this bad. There has to be something up ahead.”

As if Hell itself had finally taken pity—

“Oh!” Charlie said suddenly, voice lifting. “Look!”

Everyone leaned forward.

A flickering neon sign buzzed weakly in the distance, half its letters burned out but unmistakable all the same. DINER

Angel nearly cried. “Oh, thank every unholy thing.”

Charlie laughed in pure relief. “Yes! We can stop there. Eat, take a break— Angel, you can use the restroom.”

Angel slumped back in his seat, hand over his face. “I love you. I love that diner.”

The car turned off the main road, tires crunching over cracked pavement as the building grew closer— A lonely roadside diner, glowing warm and dingy against the endless red sky.

Alastor’s heart pounded.

A restroom.

A real one.

Hope flared in his chest so suddenly it almost undid him.

He forced himself to stay perfectly still, perfectly composed, even as relief felt achingly close. This— This could work... If he timed it right… If he moved carefully… If he let Angel rush ahead and draw all attention—

No one would notice.

He swallowed hard, smile tightening as another wave of desperation surged through him, worse than any before. His grip on the cane trembled violently now, though he kept his posture immaculate.

The car rolled to a stop.

Charlie put it in park. “Okay! Let’s all go inside. Food, bathrooms, stretch our legs.”

Angel was already fumbling with the door handle. “Bathroom first. Everything else second.”

Vaggie glanced back once more, eye locking onto Alastor. Her expression softened— Concern mixed with something like understanding. “You coming?” she asked.

Alastor inclined his head slightly. “Of course.” He rose with careful precision, every movement slow and deliberate, as though nothing were wrong at all. Inside, he was a breath away from shattering— But, outwardly, he was the same impeccable Radio Demon he had always been.

Alastor’s only thoughts were simple: ‘Please. Let me disappear into that restroom without anyone seeing just how badly I need it. And, please, please, PLEASE, let me manage to USE it!’

The diner’s bell jingled as they stepped inside, the air thick with grease, old coffee, and humming neon lights. Cracked vinyl booths, a counter with stools, and the blessed, unmistakable sign on the back wall: RESTROOMS.

Angel Dust didn’t hesitate.

“Outta my way—!” he barked, already sprinting, heels skidding slightly on the tile as he vanished through the men’s room door.

Charlie laughed weakly, relief still buzzing through her. “Wow. He really needed that…”

Vaggie shook her head. “Idiot.”

Alastor, meanwhile, paused just inside the diner, lifting a gloved hand to his brow as if suddenly struck by discomfort. “Ah… My word,” he said, voice smooth but a touch too loud, “That car WAS dreadfully stuffy. All that recycled air. I think I’ll freshen up— Perhaps wash my face.” He gestured vaguely toward the restrooms, smile fixed, posture immaculate.

Vaggie watched him for a second too long. “…Sure,” she said slowly.

Alastor turned and walked— No, STROLLED— Towards the men’s room with deliberate grace. Each step was carefully measured, every instinct screaming at him to hurry while pride forced him to maintain an unhurried pace.

‘Almost there,’ he told himself. ‘Just a few more steps. Please, let me be able to go…’

The door swung open.

Inside, the bathroom was dim, lit by a flickering fluorescent light that buzzed softly overhead. Old tile. A long mirror. Two urinals. One stall.

Angel stood at the far urinal, shoulders sagging in pure, unfiltered relief. “Ohhh,” he breathed, tension draining from him instantly. “Sweet mercy.”

The sound, unavoidable, unmistakable, hit Alastor like a physical blow.

His entire body tensed, breath catching sharply before he could stop it. The sudden proximity of relief— Someone else’s relief— Made his own need spike violently, a cruel reminder of how close he was and how little control he actually had.

He froze just inside the doorway for half a second.

Angel glanced over his shoulder, smirking. “Wow, you look like you ran a marathon in a straitjacket.”

Alastor cleared his throat, straightening. “Merely making use of the facilities,” he said lightly. “One may as well, while we’re here.”

Angel snorted. “Uh-huh. Sure.”

Alastor did not approach the urinals. That would be unthinkable.

Instead, he moved stiffly into the stall, closing the door behind him with careful precision, as though sealing himself inside a soundproof booth on his radio show.

Alone.

Finally.

He exhaled shakily, unzipping himself, willing his body to cooperate.

…

…

…

Nothing happened.

His heart raced. His muscles locked tighter instead of loosening, anxiety flooding in now that he was enclosed, aware of every sound, every echo, every second ticking by. Public restroom. Thin walls. Someone right outside.

‘Relax,’ he commanded himself, jaw clenched. ‘This is absurd. It’s only Angel Dust. You are safe.’

But, his body refused.

The pressure was unbearable— Agonizingly so— But, the tension, the awareness of being overheard, of being perceived, held him frozen in place. His breath came shallow and uneven, frustration flaring hot and sharp beneath his composure.

Outside the stall, Angel finished with a satisfied sigh. “Oh wow. I needed that.”

Alastor squeezed his eyes shut. ‘Please. I NEED to…’

Nothing.

Angel washed his hands, glancing towards the stall. “You good in there, Al?”

“Yes,” Alastor snapped automatically— Then, softened it a fraction. “Perfectly.”

Angel raised a brow. “You sure? You’re takin’ a while…”

“Patience is a virtue,” Alastor replied through clenched teeth.

Angel shrugged, drying his hands. “Hey, no judgment. Long drive does things to ya.” He headed for the door, pausing with one last sideways glance. “… You can do it, Al.”

The door swung shut behind him, leaving Alastor alone with a body that stubbornly, cruelly refused to give him the relief he needed so badly.

‘Not now,’ he thought desperately. ‘Not when I need to go THIS terribly…’

Alastor stood perfectly still, shoulders tight, every nerve alight, waiting for his body to do the one simple thing it had been screaming for all day. He focused. He breathed— Slow, controlled, counted breaths like he was calming himself before a broadcast.

Nothing.

The silence pressed in on him, heavy and accusing. The faint buzz of the fluorescent light overhead sounded impossibly loud. He was acutely aware of the thinness of the stall walls, the way sound carried in places like this. Anyone could walk in. Anyone could HEAR.

The thought alone sent a spike of panic straight through him.

His muscles locked harder instead of easing. His heart thudded painfully against his ribs, anxiety feeding the problem in a vicious loop. The more he NEEDED relief, the more impossible it became.

“This is… Ridiculous,” he muttered under his breath, voice tight.

He tried again, adjusted his stance, closed his eyes, forced himself to relax his shoulders.

Nothing.

The pressure intensified, a constant, aching insistence that made his knees feel weak. He swallowed hard, jaw clenched, humiliation burning hotter than the discomfort itself.

‘You are alone,’ he told himself. ‘No one is watching. No one cares. Everyone relieves themselves.’

But the fear didn’t listen to logic. It whispered about footsteps, about the door opening, about being overheard in a moment he could never take back. The idea of someone realizing just how desperate he was made his chest tighten until breathing felt difficult.

The harder he tried, the worse it got.

Minutes dragged by.

“I just need to go,” he murmured, to himself or to the universe, he wasn’t sure which.

His body still refused.

***

Out in the diner, Angel Dust slid into the booth. He tapped his fingers against the tabletop, eyes flicking to the restroom door for the fourth time in as many minutes.

‘Yeah,’ he thought. ‘Al’s stuck.’

Alastor was basically made of pride and anxiety wrapped in a murder smile. Public restrooms were probably his personal nightmare.

Angel groaned softly and leaned back, staring up at the flickering diner lights. He knew already that he wasn’t the right guy for this job. It was Charlie that was good at this sort of thing.

But, there were two very obvious problems.

First: How the fuck did he tell Charlie what was going on without announcing to the entire diner that the Radio Demon had bathroom-related stage-fright?

Second: Even if Charlie knew… She couldn’t exactly waltz into the men’s room.

Angel drummed his fingers faster, chewing on the inside of his cheek. ‘Okay. Think,’ he told himself. ‘You’re good at thinkin’ under pressure. Usually about crime. Or drugs. Or sex. But still.’

He glanced over at Charlie and Vaggie, who were flipping through a menu together, unaware. Charlie looked relaxed now, finally smiling again. Angel didn’t want to wipe that away, but Alastor had been gone too long.

Angel sighed. “…Alright, Al,” he murmured, eyes back on the restroom door. “I’ll get ya some help.”

***

Alastor straightened slowly, forcing his expression back into something controlled, even as frustration and misery twisted in his chest.

Still no relief, only time slipping by, and the growing, dreadful certainty that if something didn’t change, this humiliating stalemate was going to get much worse before it got better.

Alastor’s panic didn’t grow gradually. It surged.

The knowledge that he couldn’t simply wait it out— That he couldn’t last until they got back to the hotel, until he could retreat to the privacy of his own room, until he could use HIS bathroom— Was a knife twisting in his gut. Every second that passed without relief felt like a countdown. He knew the limits of his own body, the cruel truth that if he held on too long, he would not just suffer— He would make himself sick.

‘You HAVE to go,’ his mind insisted. ‘You must.’

And still… Nothing.

His muscles clenched with desperate force, the way they did when he was trying to choke out a scream. His breathing grew shallow, quick, irregular. His mind kept spiraling, because once panic took hold, it fed itself. ‘What if someone comes in? What if they hear me? What if they know? What if I can’t do this and I have to go back out there like this?’

The fear of being seen in that moment was more humiliating than the discomfort itself.

***

Angel sat at the booth, trying not to look frantic while his eyes kept flicking to the bathroom door. He had never felt so useless in his life. It wasn’t like he couldn’t help— He just couldn’t help in the right way.

He looked over at Charlie, who was still holding a menu. He leaned over, voice low. “Charlie,” he said, “You know how Al… uh… How his ‘pipes’ get when he’s in a public place?”

Charlie blinked, instantly understanding. “Oh.”

Angel nodded, relieved she got it. “Yeah. He’s… He’s stuck. Like, really stuck. He needs… A push.”

Charlie’s expression tightened with concern. “Oh no. How long has he been needing to—”

“Long enough that I’m pretty sure he’s dying,” Angel said bluntly. “He’s in there trying, but he’s not— He’s not doing it.”

Charlie’s face softened. “Okay.”

Angel leaned closer, lowering his voice even more. “So like… How do we help him?”

Charlie’s shoulders sagged. “I wish I could. But I can’t go in there.”

Angel grimaced. “Yeah. That’s a problem.”

Charlie looked around the diner quickly, eyes landing on the restroom sign. “This is why most of Hell has unisex bathrooms,” she murmured. “Because you never know who’s going to need help from who.”

Vaggie raised an eyebrow. “Uh, babe, I don’t think THAT’S why…”

Angel leaned forward, eyes narrowing. “So what do we do?”

Charlie glanced at the restroom door again, then back at Angel, thinking hard. “Okay,” she said. “We need someone who can do the grounding exercise with him.“ She looked towards Husk. “Husk,” she said, “Can you help Alastor?”

Husk blinked slowly. “Me?”

Charlie nodded. “I can teach you the steps. Just… Guide him through it.”

Husk’s expression shifted from confusion to reluctant understanding. “You want me to… Do a calming thing. With Alastor.”

“Exactly,” Charlie said. “Just… Talk to him. Tell him to focus on his breathing. Then get him to feel his feet on the floor. The goal is to get his mind out of the fear loop.”

Husk nodded, already standing. “Alright, fine,” he said, voice low. “I’m goin’ in.”

Angel watched him go, then turned to Charlie. “You sure this’ll work?”

Charlie bit her lip, staring at the restroom door as if willing it to open on its own. “I hope so,” she whispered. “Because if it doesn’t… I don’t know what we’re going to do.”

Husk pushed open the bathroom door and stepped inside.

Alastor’s head snapped up at the sound of the door opening, eyes wide with sudden alarm. Then he recognized Husk’s footsteps and, just as quickly, his posture shifted. His shoulders dropped a fraction. The panic in his eyes didn’t vanish, but it softened.

“Husker,” Alastor said, voice tight but controlled. “What are you doing in here?”

Husk leaned against the wall, arms crossed. “Don’t worry. I’m not here to stare. I’m here to help.”

Alastor blinked, startled. That was… Not the kind of sentence Alastor expected to hear from Husk.“I… Appreciate that,” Alastor said carefully, still holding himself rigidly. “But I assure you, I am quite capable.”

Husk snorted. “Yeah, well. You’ve been in there a while. And I’ve seen you dealin’ with this before. You’re not capable of shit.”

Alastor’s cheeks flushed faintly. He opened his mouth to argue, then closed it again.

Husk stepped closer, keeping his voice low and steady. “Okay. Here’s the deal. I’m gonna help you breathe. That’s it. No jokes. No snark. Just… Breathe.”

Alastor’s eyes widened again, and he gave a small, incredulous laugh— An involuntary sound, half amusement, half humiliation. “You’re serious?”

Husk shrugged, looking mildly uncomfortable. “Yeah. I’m serious.“

Alastor hesitated for a moment, then nodded slowly. “Very well. I suppose… I have no choice.”

Husk blinked. ‘He’s actually accepting this just like that?’ he thought. ‘How bad does he need to piss?’

A strange feeling settled in Husk’s chest—Something like pity, mixed with the ridiculousness of the situation. Alastor, the Radio Demon, the terror of Hell, was standing in a public restroom with his face drawn tight and his body trembling because he couldn’t pee.

Husk felt a little silly. And yet… He didn’t want Alastor to hurt himself.

He cleared his throat, then spoke in a calm, almost gentle tone. “Alright,” he said. “First thing: Breathe with me. In through the nose. Hold it for two seconds. Out through the mouth.”

Alastor followed, stiff at first, then gradually loosening a fraction. Husk counted softly, steady and patient, like he was coaxing a stubborn animal. “In… Two… Three. Out.”

Alastor’s breath shook on the exhale. His eyes were still wide, panic still simmering under the surface, but the rhythm was changing.

Husk continued. “Okay. Again. In. Hold. Out.”

Alastor did it again, slower this time, his shoulders dropping a fraction more.

Husk was still feeling oddly awkward. He wasn’t used to helping anyone like this. And, Alastor wasn’t exactly the type to receive kindness without mocking it.

“Now,” Husk said, “Keep your feet flat on the floor. Feel the tile. Feel the weight of your body.”

Alastor stared at his feet for a moment, then complied, pressing them down, grounding himself.

Husk continued, voice steady. “Focus on the feeling. Not on what’s happening. Just… The sensation of the floor under your feet.”

Alastor’s breathing continued, slow and controlled.

Husk kept going, and with each breath, Alastor seemed to relax a fraction more. The panic didn’t disappear, but it stopped spiraling.

Husk’s own discomfort faded as he focused on the task. He didn’t want to make a spectacle of Alastor, and he didn’t want to let him suffer.

He just wanted him to be okay.

“Alright,” Husk said quietly. “Now— Try again. But don’t force it. Just… Let it happen.”

Alastor swallowed hard, closing his eyes. For a moment, his face twisted in pain, then he nodded. “I’m trying,” he whispered, voice strained.

Husk’s jaw tightened. “I know you are.”

A long silence stretched between them— Broken only by the faint buzz of the light and the sound of Alastor’s controlled breathing.

Husk stayed there, patient, keeping his voice low and steady, doing something he’d never imagined he’d do for the Radio Demon. He felt a strange sense of responsibility now. Not because he liked Alastor. Certainly not because he trusted him. But, because nobody deserved to hurt like this. especially not for something so basic.

And because, in that moment, Husk realized that even a jerk like Alastor could be… Human. Or at least, as human as a demon could be.

Husk wasn’t a therapist. He wasn’t a counselor. He was a grumpy bartender with a bottle and a bitter outlook on life.

But, he knew people.

He knew how to read them. He knew how to make them laugh, or forget, or focus on something else long enough to get through a bad patch.

And he knew, from years of watching drunkards and broken hearts and desperate sinners, that sometimes the best way to pull someone out of panic was to distract them with a story.

So Husk cleared his throat, then began. “Alright,” he said, voice low and steady. “You want a story?”

Alastor’s eyes narrowed. “A story?”

Husk nodded. “Yeah. One of those old bar stories. You know. Something to keep your mind off the fact that you’re about to lose your dignity.”

“… Fine.”

Husk took a breath, then started. “Okay. So there was this bar,” he began, “Big, ugly place. No windows. Smelled like smoke and regret. And this one night, this… Real troublemaker walks in. This guy, he’s loud. He’s nasty. He thinks he owns the place. He orders a drink, then he starts talkin’ to everyone like he’s the king of the world. And the bartender, he’s just watchin’ him. Waitin’. Because he knows— He knows what kind of guy this is.”

Alastor’s breathing remained controlled, but the panic in his eyes softened a fraction.

Husk’s voice grew quieter, more intimate, like he was telling the story to someone sitting at the bar in the dark. “So the troublemaker starts gettin’ more and more arrogant. He starts threatenin’ people. He starts callin’ folks names. He’s makin’ a mess.”

Alastor’s lips pressed together.

“And the bartender?” Husk continued. “He doesn’t say a word. He just keeps pourin’ drinks. Keeps watchin’. Keeps his calm.”

Alastor’s shoulders loosened slightly, just a little.

“Then the troublemaker does the thing he always does,” Husk said. “He tries to start some fights, thinks he’s invincible. He thinks nobody can stop him.” Husk paused.

Alastor’s breathing slowed. He was focusing now. Listening.

“And then,” Husk said, “The bartender says somethin’ real quiet. Something so quiet the troublemaker has to lean in to hear it. And he says, ‘You know what? I’ve seen your type before. I’ve seen the way you act when you think nobody’s watchin.’” And the troublemaker, he’s like, ‘Oh yeah? And what?’ And the bartender says, ‘I’ve seen you get scared.’”

Alastor’s shoulders twitched.

Husk waited a beat, then finished. “And the troublemaker, he just… Freezes. Because the bartender didn’t threaten him. He didn’t fight him. He didn’t try to humiliate him. He just said the one thing that makes every tough guy collapse.” Husk’s voice softened further. “He said the thing that makes you realize you’re not the only one with fear.”

Alastor’s breathing had slowed now, and his body was still tense, but the panic had loosened a fraction.

“And then the troublemaker… He started cryin’. Not because he was weak. Because he finally felt safe enough to let go.”

Alastor’s eyes flickered shut. He trembled. The pressure surged again.

And then— Suddenly— Without warning—Alastor’s body unclenched.

The floodgates opened.

A rush of relief so intense it felt like electricity surged through him, and he finally— FINALLY— Began to urinate.

The sound filled the restroom, loud and unstoppable.

Alastor’s eyes flew open, bliss washing over his face like a wave.

He let out a shaky breath, voice barely above a whisper. “Ohhhh…”

He went and went, the strain easing from his body, the desperate tension dissolving with each passing second. His shoulders sagged, his hands relaxed, and the pain that had been clawing at him for so long finally began to fade.

It was overwhelming.

He didn’t just feel better— He felt alive.

Husk stood there quietly, a strange mix of satisfaction and mild embarrassment on his face.

When it finally stopped, Alastor leaned back against the stall wall, eyes closed, breathing heavy but calm. He looked… Exhausted. But relieved.

Husk cleared his throat. “You good?” he asked quietly.

Alastor opened the stall door slowly and nodded, still trying to process the flood of relief. “Yes,” he said hoarsely. “Yes. Thank you.”

Husk shrugged, looking away. “Don’t make a habit of it.”

Alastor managed a small, almost sheepish smile. “I won’t.”

***

Alastor emerged from the bathroom with the kind of composure he always wore like a mask, except now, it wasn’t just a mask.

It was genuine.

His posture was relaxed. His shoulders were down. His breathing was normal again. His smile was back in place, bright and smug and unmistakably him.

Husk followed behind, the slight stiffness in his shoulders betraying the fact that he’d just done something far outside his comfort zone.

As they stepped into the diner’s main room, the atmosphere changed.

Charlie looked up immediately, eyes wide with relief. “Alastor! Are you okay?”

Vaggie’s gaze sharpened, then softened as she took in his relaxed posture. “He looks… Normal.”

Angel, who was already halfway through a plate of fries, sat up straighter. “You good, Al?”

Alastor flashed them all a grin. “My dears,” he said, voice smooth as silk, “I am quite splendid, thank you.”

Charlie let out a breath. “Oh thank goodness.”

Angel leaned back, a grin tugging at his lips. “See? Told ya you could do it.”

Vaggie crossed her arms, trying not to smile. “Just don’t make a habit of disappearing into the bathroom for twenty minutes.”

Alastor’s grin widened. “I can’t promise that.”

Husk cleared his throat, shifting his weight. “He’s fine now.”

Charlie turned toward Husk, eyes grateful. “Thank you.”

Husk shrugged, trying to look unimpressed. “Don’t make it weird.”

Charlie smiled. “I won’t.”

Alastor leaned into the booth, his expression smug, eyes bright and sharp. “Now,” he said, voice smooth and theatrical, “Where were we?”

Angel laughed, waving a fry in the air. “Right here, buddy. Right here. And you can sit there lookin’ like you didn’t just nearly die in a public restroom.”

Alastor chuckled softly. “I assure you, I did not nearly die. I merely experienced a moment of inconvenience.”

Charlie’s shoulders relaxed completely now, the tension draining from her face. “I’m just glad you’re okay.”

Alastor’s grin softened, just slightly. “As am I, my dear.”
