Alastor stood near the lobby’s edge, hands folded neatly behind his back, smile fixed in its usual razor-sharp crescent. To any observer, he appeared perfectly composed: The Radio Demon at rest, observing the room like a host listening to a program he’d already memorized.

Internally, however, irritation crackled louder than any broadcast.

He needed to use the restroom.

It was an inconvenience, nothing more. A minor biological necessity that any other day, he’d be able to handle here with swift, private efficiency. Unfortunately, privacy was in short supply today, even here at the hotel, with his room right upstairs. The issue was the floating, blinking camera drone hovering several feet above his head.

One of Vox’s stupid surveillance devices. Alastor should have expected this after the fiasco the other day. Vox was… Suspicious. He knew Alastor was hiding SOMETHING, but, blessedly, he didn’t have any clear ideas of WHAT it was.

Hence the camera.

Vox clearly intended to watch Alastor until he had gathered enough evidence to form some new theories.

Alastor refused to provide any.

But, if the camera didn’t leave him alone soon, he may be forced to. It was only a matter of time before the pressure inside him made him squirm visibly, notifying Vox of the fullness in his bladder. And, if he tried to satiate his need with the camera on him, he already knew what would happen; Complete and total lock-up.

The camera would broadcast that to Vox; Alastor struggling urgently in front of the toilet, nothing pouring from him. That would be MORE than enough evidence. Vox would know everything!

So, Alastor just had to hold it— Without fidgeting too obviously— Until he got the camera away from him.

The device buzzed softly, adjusting its angle.

Alastor’s eye twitched.

“Ah… How charming,” he said aloud, voice smooth, though pitched just low enough to sound like a threat. “Do run along now, won’t you? You’ve had your fill of my good side.”

The camera didn’t respond. It merely zoomed in a fraction, its red lens dilating with unmistakable interest.

Alastor turned slowly, smile never faltering, and stared directly into the lens. The air around him crackled faintly with radio static, an old signal struggling against modern interference.

“Really, my dear television tyrant,” he continued, adjusting his coat with meticulous care, “Must you hover so? One would think you’d never seen a demon walk around before.”

The camera followed as he began to move.

Alastor strode through the lobby, cane tapping rhythmically against the floor. Each step was measured, unhurried— Despite the growing urgency he refused to acknowledge openly. The camera kept pace, floating just off his shoulder, occasionally darting ahead to catch his expression from the front.

He stopped abruptly.

The camera overshot him by a foot, then corrected itself.

Alastor leaned in close, grin widening just enough to show teeth. “You know,” he said pleasantly, “There are limits to my hospitality.”

Static hissed in the air, the lights flickering for half a second. The camera wobbled but didn’t retreat.

Somewhere, he knew Vox was enjoying this.

Alastor straightened with a sharp inhale, composure snapping back into place like a well-tuned dial. Fine. If discretion was unavailable, then misdirection would have to do.

He turned down a side hallway— One less traveled, dimmer, lined with doors that led to storage rooms and unused suites. The camera followed faithfully, bobbing along like an eager parasite.

“Honestly,” Alastor muttered, “One cannot even attend to the simplest personal matters without an audience these days.”

He paused at a closed door, fingers tightening subtly around his cane. The smile never left his face, but something colder flashed behind his eyes.

“This,” he announced cheerfully, gesturing to the hallway, “Is where your little program ends.”

The camera tilted, as if curious.

The lights flickered again.

Alastor inhaled slowly through his nose, posture still immaculate, but the rhythm of his cane tapping against the floor had changed. Faster now. Less musical.

The camera hovered.

Watching.

Waiting.

“Well then,” Alastor said lightly, turning on his heel and continuing down the corridor, “If you insist on being such a devoted listener, who am I to deny you a show?”

He picked up his pace.

The hallway seemed longer than it had any right to be. Each step echoed, stretching time in a way Alastor found deeply offensive. His usual swagger tightened into something sharper, more controlled, as though he were holding himself together through sheer force of will.

Behind him, the camera zipped closer, lens whirring as it adjusted focus.

Alastor stopped again— This time abruptly enough that his coat tails swayed forward.

The camera nearly collided with him.

In a flash, Alastor spun and swung his cane.

There was a burst of static, radio waves screaming through the air like a blown speaker. The cane struck the camera dead-on with enough force to shatter stone.

The camera dented.

And then… Righted itself.

Its lens flickered once… And it resumed hovering.

Alastor stared at it.

Silence.

Then, a soft, crackling laugh escaped him, strained at the edges. “Oh, how delightful. Reinforced.”

He rolled his shoulders, visibly restraining something far less polite than words. The pressure of the moment— Physical and otherwise— Was becoming increasingly difficult to ignore. He shifted his stance, adjusting his grip on the cane, jaw tightening for just a fraction of a second before the smile snapped back into place.

“Very well,” he said, voice pitching into exaggerated cheer. “Let’s try something a bit more… Theatrical.”

The hallway lights exploded.

Darkness swallowed the corridor, broken only by pulsing radio waves that rippled outward from Alastor like sound made visible. The walls distorted, stretching and bending as though reality itself were tuning to his frequency.

For a moment, JUST a moment, the camera lost altitude, spinning erratically as its stabilizers fought the interference.

Alastor didn’t waste the opportunity.

He bolted.

Well… Strode extremely quickly, dignity held together by a thread.

He ducked through a doorway, slammed it shut, and twisted the lock with a sharp snap of his fingers. The room beyond was a storage space, filled with old furniture and dusty props. He leaned briefly against the door, exhaling through clenched teeth.

“…Unbelievable,” he muttered.

Silence.

He straightened, smoothing his coat, already preparing to move again. ‘Now, if I can just get to my room…’

Buzz.

The sound came from above.

Alastor twitched as he looked up just in time to see the camera descend from an air vent, slightly scuffed, lens bright with triumph.

“Oh, you insufferable fucking—“

He caught himself, straightening sharply, as though dignity alone could keep the situation under control. The urgency had escalated from inconvenient to dire, every second stretching thinner than the last. He shifted again, clearly struggling to maintain his necessary stillness.

The camera zoomed in.

Alastor planted his cane against the floor with enough force to splinter the tile. “I am warning you,” he said softly, voice buzzing with distorted radio feedback, “There are some lines even you should know better than to cross.”

Static answered him.

Then, faintly, mockingly, Vox’s voice crackled through the camera’s speaker.

“Aw, what’s wrong, Alastor?” it drawled. “You don’t want to spend time with me?”

Alastor’s eye flared red.

He turned sharply and marched for the opposite door. The camera followed, weaving around him like a smug little mosquito. In the hall, he yanked open doors at random— Closets, empty rooms, a laundry area— Slamming each shut when it failed to offer sanctuary.

“This is harassment,” he snapped, composure finally fraying. “You DO realize that, don’t you?”

“Funny,” Vox replied, amused. “I was thinking the same thing about your mere existence!”

Alastor stopped dead in the middle of the hall.

For a moment, the hotel itself seemed to hold its breath.

He straightened slowly, smile returning, wider, sharper, but undeniably strained. “Oh, Vox,” he said sweetly, “When I’m finished here… We are going to have a very long conversation.”

The camera hovered, unafraid.

And Alastor, running out of time, turned towards the last door at the end of the corridor— One he’d been deliberately avoiding.

A bathroom.

His fingers tightened around the handle.

The camera drifted closer.

Alastor paused with his hand on the door, then very deliberately relaxed his posture.

The tension didn’t vanish, but it hid itself behind poise, behind performance. He straightened his coat, lifted his chin, and turned to face the hovering camera with renewed calm.

“Oh dear me,” he said lightly, as though he hadn’t just been tearing through half the hotel. “If you’re quite finished skulking about like a roach with a lens, I was just about to indulge in a bath.”

The camera drifted closer, lens narrowing.

“A bath?” Vox echoed, skeptical amusement crackling through the speaker. “It’s the middle of the day.”

“Indeed,” Alastor replied smoothly. “Even demons of culture require time to unwind. Soaking. Silence. Solitude.” He gestured to the door with exaggerated elegance. “All things you are currently ruining.”

He waited.

The camera didn’t move.

Instead, it tilted, scanning him slowly from head to toe, lingering just a fraction too long.

Alastor’s smile stiffened.

“Go ahead then,” Vox said.

Alastor’s eye twitched. “I hate being watched,” he snapped, then immediately smoothed his tone. “Now really, Vox. Surely even YOU understand the concept of privacy. Or has that been edited out of your programming?”

Static crackled, followed by a low chuckle. “Funny thing about privacy,” Vox said. “It’s usually what people ask for when they’ve got something to hide.”

The camera inched closer.

Alastor shifted his weight, subtly, but unmistakably. His grip tightened around his cane again, knuckles whitening beneath his gloves. The effort it took to remain still was starting to show, like a radio signal stretched too thin.

He laughed— A little too quickly. “Hide? Please. I’m merely trying to enjoy a quiet soak without an audience of blinking eyes and buzzing motors.”

The camera’s red lens brightened.

“Then why do you look so shifty?” Vox asked.

For half a second, the hallway lights dimmed.

Alastor leaned forward, smile razor-wide, voice dropping into a dangerous hum layered with static. “Mind your tone.”

The pressure— Physical, mental, and deeply humiliating— Was reaching its limit. Every instinct screamed at him to move, to finish this, to make the problem go away before dignity followed patience into oblivion. He tapped his cane once. Twice. Too fast.

The camera noticed.

“Oh,” Vox said, delighted. “Ohhh. Somebody’s nervous!”

Alastor straightened abruptly. “That’s quite enough,” he said, clipped and tight. “I am taking a bath. Alone. If you insist on lingering, I will—“ He stopped himself, inhaled sharply, and forced the words back into a smoother cadence. “—I will consider it a personal provocation.”

The camera hovered in place.

Then, slowly, it drifted past him— Sliding right up to the bathroom door and turning back to face him, as if inviting him to try.

Alastor stared. His smile was still there, but it had gone brittle at the edges. “… Vox,” he said. “I’m not going to strip in front of you. Ever. You can forget about that.”

Vox laughed openly now, the sound buzzing through the speaker. “Relax, Radio Star. What’s the harm? You said it yourself— Just a bath.”

Alastor’s eyes flared again as he took a stiff step toward the door. He hesitated, body betraying him with another subtle shift, another fraction of lost control. The camera zoomed in slightly.

No.

Absolutely not.

He turned away from the bathroom with a sharp pivot, stalking down the hall again— Pace quicker than before, dignity unraveling with every step.

The camera zipped after him, triumphant.

“New plan,” Alastor muttered under his breath. “New plan, new plan…”

He ducked into another corridor, then another, trying doors. His breathing stayed measured, but his movements had grown tight, clipped, like a broadcast about to cut out mid-sentence.

“This is ridiculous,” he hissed, rounding a corner only to find the camera already there, floating smugly at eye level.

Vox’s voice crackled warmly. “You know, Alastor… I wouldn’t MIND watching you take a bath.”

Alastor stopped. Slowly, he lifted his head, grin returning in full, brilliant force. But there was something frantic under it now, something sharp and cracking through the radio static. “Oh, Vox,” he said, voice buzzing. “When this is over… You will regret every second of this broadcast.”

The camera hovered, utterly unafraid.

Alastor had reached a point he deeply resented.

Every muscle in his body was locked in an iron grip of restraint, posture held together by habit and pride alone. Standing still was agony. Moving was worse. The pressure was no longer something he could ignore. It demanded attention with every second, every heartbeat, every humiliating reminder that he was not, in fact, above basic physical needs.

He took a careful step forward.

Then another.

The camera drifted alongside him, perfectly matched, its lens trained on his face like a spotlight he couldn’t escape.

Alastor clenched his jaw.

‘Do not squirm. Do not rush. Do not—‘

He stopped abruptly, pressing his cane firmly to the floor as though anchoring himself there. The sudden stillness made his breath hitch, just slightly. He recovered quickly, straightening with forced elegance, but the strain was unmistakable now— His smile stretched too tight, his movements just a beat off their usual rhythm.

“This,” he said through a pleasant tone that took far too much effort to maintain, “Is rapidly becoming intolerable.”

Vox hummed thoughtfully through the speaker. “Al, if you keep pacing like that, people might start to think you’re nervous.”

“Nonsense,” Alastor snapped, then smoothed it over with a laugh that crackled with static.

He turned sharply and ducked behind a heavy velvet curtain. For a brief, glorious moment, the camera overshot him.

Alastor pressed himself into the shadowed corner, one gloved hand gripping the fabric, the other clenched around his cane. He forced himself to stand perfectly still, every instinct screaming at him to move, to shift, to do something.

The buzzing grew louder.

The curtain lifted.

The camera slid in sideways, lens glowing smugly in the dim light.

Alastor’s composure fractured.

A sharp, involuntary movement betrayed him—Just a small, desperate adjustment of his stance when his bladder surged violently against his aching opening— But, it was enough. He froze immediately, eyes wide.

“…You,” he said softly, voice trembling with radio distortion, “Are enjoying this far too much.”

Vox laughed. “Oh, absolutely.”

Alastor lunged forward, then immediately moved— No, practically sprinted— Down the hall before the camera could reposition. His steps were faster now, uneven despite his best efforts, cane tapping erratically instead of rhythmically.

He burst into a supply closet and slammed the door behind him, fumbling with the lock.

“Stay. Still,” he muttered to himself, shoulders tense, breath shallow. He leaned back against the door, every ounce of willpower focused on not pacing the tiny space.

Silence.

Five seconds passed.

Then the light flickered.

A tiny red glow appeared above him.

Alastor looked up just in time to see the camera peering down through a grated ceiling vent.

His eye twitched violently.

“Fuck,” he snarled, finally dropping the polite cadence entirely.

Vox’s voice drifted down, delighted. “You really should’ve checked the ceiling.”

Alastor pushed off the door and staggered forward a step, catching himself on a shelf. His breath came sharper now, control slipping no matter how hard he tried to hold it. He straightened again immediately, but the damage was done— His stillness had been replaced by barely restrained squirming, knees locked, shoulders tense, every line of him screaming urgency.

He fled the closet.

Down another hall. Around a corner. Behind a pillar. Under a stairwell.

Each hiding place bought him seconds at most before the camera found a new angle, a new vent, a new impossible line of sight. Vox’s laughter followed him everywhere, buzzing through speakers.

Alastor stopped beneath the staircase, hunched slightly before catching himself and standing straight again, hands trembling just enough to be noticeable.

“I will not,” he hissed, “Be undone by a floating box with an ego.”

The camera hovered just outside the stairwell, lens fixed on him.

Alastor’s smile flickered, then steadied, though sweat beaded faintly at his temple. He glanced up, then to the narrow space beneath the stairs, eyes narrowing.

And Alastor realized, with a spike of near-panic, that he was running out of places to hide— And TIME to pretend this was anything other than exactly what it was.

Alastor COULD NOT do this anymore.

The pressure had reached a point where sheer willpower was the only thing keeping him upright, let alone composed. Every step now required focus. Every pause threatened disaster. His usual elegance had narrowed into something sharp and frantic, his movements clipped as though the world itself were conspiring to slow him down.

He caught sight of it at the end of a rarely used service hall.

A door.

Small.

Unmarked.

Alastor’s eye lit up with something close to salvation.

The supply closet was infamous among the hotel staff— Too narrow to be useful, too cramped to store much of anything. Too warm. No vents. No windows. No gaps beneath the door.

Perfect.

Alastor HURRIED, no longer pretending this was a casual walk. His cane tapped rapidly, nearly out of sync with his steps as he reached the door and fumbled with the knob. His hand shook just enough to betray him, but the latch gave way with a soft click.

He slipped inside and slammed the door shut behind him.

Darkness.

Silence.

No buzzing.

No hovering.

No red lens.

For the first time in what felt like an eternity, Alastor was alone.

He sagged back against the door, every ounce of control straining to its limit. His breathing came quick and shallow as he pressed his cane to the floor, using it to steady himself.

“…Finally,” he whispered, voice trembling.

Outside, the faint buzz of the camera could be heard zipping past the door, confused. It circled once. Twice. Then stopped.

Vox’s voice crackled faintly. “Huh. Where’d you go?”

Alastor squeezed his eyes shut and said nothing.

Seconds ticked by. Each one felt dangerously thin. He clenched his jaw, posture rigid, every instinct screaming for relief while he fought to keep himself together. The effort showed now— Shoulders tight, fingers curled, feet rocking.

Outside, the camera bumped gently against the door.

Once.

Twice.

It didn’t fit.

There was no vent to peer through. No crack beneath the frame. No angle to exploit.

Static hissed in irritation. “…That’s not fair,” Vox muttered.

Alastor let out a shaky, breathless laugh— Quiet, victorious, and just a little unhinged. He pressed his forehead briefly against the door, eyes glowing faintly in the dark.

“Oh,” he murmured, voice low and crackling, “I assure you… It’s entirely fair.”

The camera buzzed angrily on the other side, scraping uselessly against solid wood.

For the first time since this nightmare had begun, Alastor allowed himself a fragile spark of hope. He was hidden. Truly hidden. Just long enough— PLEASE, just long enough— To make it through this with what little dignity he had left.

Outside, Vox went quiet.

Alastor clung to control by the thinnest possible thread— Heart pounding, time stretched to its limit, praying the universe would grant him mercy before that thread snapped.

The moment the buzzing outside finally drifted away, something in Alastor gave.

Not completely, but enough that the rigid statue he’d forced himself to be finally cracked.

He shifted.

Just a little at first.

Then more.

He drew in a sharp breath and immediately clamped his mouth shut, one gloved hand flying to grip onto his private area, squeezing to shove some of the urgency back. His knees bent, then crossed, swaying from foot to foot with a hiss of static leaking from his throat.

“…Control,” he muttered, voice tight and trembling despite himself. “Maintain control.”

But with privacy— REAL privacy— Came the backlash of everything he’d been suppressing. Alastor had to GO. He had to go so terribly! His whole lower body was alight with need, every raw nerve pulsing and begging for a reprieve from the awful pressure.

He paced the length of the closet, which amounted to precisely three steps before he had to turn around. His movements were restless now, no longer elegant: Shifting his weight from side to side, gripping his private area, releasing it, then gripping it again. His legs rubbed together, betraying what his face refused to show. ‘Hold it, Alastor! Hold it, hold it, hold it!’

Alastor pressed his back to the wall, knees locked together for a moment before he forced them apart again, breathing uneven.

“This is beneath me,” he growled softly. “Absolutely beneath me.”

The pressure was relentless, bordering on overwhelming, and every instinct screamed at him to do something— ANYTHING— To ease it. He doubled over, then immediately straightened, teeth clenched hard enough to spark static.

‘Goodness! This is getting worse by the second!’

“Oh dear, oh dear, oh dear!”

His glowing eyes swept the cramped space, searching.

Cleaning rags. Broken broom handle. Angel Dust’s porn magazines. And—

He froze.

In the corner, half-hidden behind the pornography, sat a decorative vase. Tall. Narrow. Ceramic.

Alastor stared at it.

The silence stretched.

Slowly, he turned his head away, as though offended by the very IDEA.

“Ha! No,” he said firmly. “Absolutely not.”

He took a step away from it— And immediately regretted it, stopping short and stiffening again, breath hitching as another wave of urgency hit. His hands flew between his legs, pinching himself shut as he bounced rapidly.

“…I will not be reduced to that,” he insisted, though his voice wavered.

He glanced back at the vase.

Then away.

Then back again.

His jaw tightened, pride warring viciously with necessity. The Radio Demon, reduced to considering a vase like some undignified delinquent? No one would ever let him hear the end of it— IF they ever found out.

Alastor straightened with visible effort, forcing his posture tall despite the way his legs trembled. “I can wait,” he told himself. “I’ve waited this long.”

The words rang hollow.

He shifted again, unable to stop himself now, one foot tapping sharply against the floor before he stilled it with a growl. His breathing had gone shallow, control fraying at the edges like an old broadcast losing signal.

The vase sat there.

Silent.

Unjudging.

Waiting.

Alastor squeezed his eyes shut for a brief moment, radio static crackling faintly around his antlers.

“…This,” he whispered darkly, “Is the worst day I’ve had in decades.”

Alastor stood on the brink— Caught between pride and desperation, privacy finally secured but dignity hanging by a thread.

And time, cruel as ever, continued to tick.

Alastor stood there for several long, miserable seconds, locked in a battle that he was rapidly losing.

He tried pacing again— Three steps, turn, three steps— But, it only made things worse. He stopped, bent slightly at the waist, then straightened with a sharp inhale, offended by his own body’s betrayal. The static around him crackled faintly, erratic, mirroring the state of his nerves.

“No,” he muttered, more pleading now than commanding. “No, no, no— This will NOT be how— I will NOT—“

Another wave hit, stronger than the last, and his knees buckled just enough that he had to catch himself against the wall. Liquid slammed so aggressively inside him that he whimpered.

There was no more pretending.

The truth settled in with humiliating clarity: Vox wasn’t going to give up. The camera would be waiting the moment he stepped out. There would be no dignified stroll to his restroom, no locking the door, no mercy.

And he was out of time.

Alastor slowly turned his head to the vase again.

It hadn’t changed. Still sitting there like a silent witness to his downfall.

He stared at it for a long moment, shoulders trembling— Not with fear, but with restrained panic and wounded pride.

“…I despise you,” he said quietly, though it was unclear whether he meant the vase, his bladder, Vox, or the situation itself.

With movements that were stiff and careful, he reached for his zipper His hands shook, just slightly. He plucked up the vase and positioned its opening right over his tip, wary of ‘missing’. He paused once more, eyes squeezed shut, jaw tight.

“This,” he said under his breath, voice cracking with static, “NEVER leaves this room.”

Then, finally— FINALLY— He let go.

The relief was immediate and overwhelming, hitting him so hard that he had to brace himself against the wall to even stay upright. He exhaled sharply, head tipping forward as tension drained from him in a way he hadn’t realized was even possible. Every muscle that had been clenched for far too long slowly loosened.

For a brief, traitorous moment, all he could feel was relief.

“Ahhhhhhhhh… Mmmmm…”

Then came the shame. He couldn’t believe he was actually doing this… It was so undignified! But, it felt so, so good… Oh, he’d NEEDED this! The waves pouring out of him made him shiver, his entire body awash in blissful pleasure, even as the blush scorched his face and the embarrassment made his static ripple.

When he was finished at last, Alastor straightened, breathing uneven, face burning with indignation. He refused to look at the full vase, refused to acknowledge what he’d just been forced to do to it. His posture snapped back into place through sheer stubbornness alone, dignity stitched together with anger and denial.

“…Unacceptable,” he whispered, smoothing his coat with hands that still trembled faintly. “Absolutely, categorically unacceptable.”

But it was done.

He was still hidden. Still alone. And, most importantly, he was blessedly empty.

Alastor leaned back against the wall, eyes glowing faintly in the dark, expression torn between furious humiliation and exhausted relief.

The Radio Demon silently swore vengeance— On Vox, on surveillance technology, and on ever letting himself be pushed this far again.

He took a few deep, slow breaths and the crackling static around him evened out into a familiar, controlled hum. He glanced once— ONLY ONCE— To the vase, then lifted his cane and tapped it lightly against the floor.

The shadows in the closet shifted. Light rippled out in a tight, precise wave, and with a soft hiss— Like a signal cutting cleanly to silence— The evidence vanished. The vase was left exactly as it had been before: Dry, spotless, untouched, as though reality itself had politely agreed NOT to remember what had happened.

Alastor straightened fully.

Composure snapped back into place as if it had never left. His smile broadened, sharp and effortless. His posture was flawless again— No tension, no strain, no urgency lurking beneath the surface.

Power thrummed through him, rich and complete.

“Muuuuch better,” he said lightly, adjusting his gloves.

He slipped the vase back where he’d found it, gave the cramped closet one last disdainful look, and opened the door.

The hallway lights flickered as he stepped out.

Immediately, the familiar buzz returned.

The camera zipped into view from around the corner, hovering at eye level like an overeager insect. Its lens focused sharply on his face, searching for cracks that were no longer there.

“Well, well,” Vox’s voice chimed smugly through the speaker. “There you are. Vanish again and I might start to think you’re camera- shy.”

Alastor froze, static pulses thrumming beneath his skin. That was… TOO close. Too close to the truth.

He smiled wider.

“Oh, hardly,” he replied smoothly, twirling his cane once as he strolled on at an unhurried pace. “I just needed a break from your nonsense. Now, if you’ll excuse me, I would still very much like to take a bath— Unobserved.”

The camera drifted closer, suspicious.

“A bath,” Vox repeated. “You sure like baths today.”

Alastor stopped. He turned and looked directly into the lens. “Yes,” he said pleasantly. “I do.”

The air changed.

The lights dimmed— Not flickering this time, but bowing. The static deepened into a powerful, steady broadcast that rolled through the corridor like a wave. Alastor’s shadow stretched tall and monstrous against the wall, antlers brushing the ceiling as symbols spiraled outwards.

The camera froze midair.

“What— Hey—” Vox started.

Alastor lifted his cane.

“Goodbye,” he said cheerfully, voice layered with a hundred overlapping radio frequencies. Now that he was finished attending to his personal affairs, his powers seemed to all be at full strength once more.

He tapped the cane once.

Reality screamed.

A concentrated burst of static slammed into the camera, crushing it inward with a shriek of warped sound and sparks. The lens shattered, the casing imploded, and the machine collapsed in on itself before detonating into harmless static that dissolved into the air.

Silence.

Vox’s connection cut off mid-sputter.

Alastor lowered his cane, straightened his coat, and smiled— Bright, victorious, and entirely composed.

“There,” he said softly. “Privacy restored.”

He turned and walked calmly down the hall, every step measured, dignity intact, as though nothing humiliating had ever occurred.

And, Vox would never, EVER know the truth.

The chandeliers still hummed faintly with electricity, the carpets still swallowed footsteps whole, and Hell itself still roared beyond the hotel’s walls…

But, HIS sound was gone.

Alastor stood alone in the lobby.

Morning filtered through the tall, arched windows in a dull crimson haze. Dust motes drifted lazily in the air, illuminated like embers suspended in time. The Radio Demon was perfectly still, hands clasped behind his back, posture impeccable as ever. His grin was there, carved sharp and familiar into his face, all pointed teeth and cheerfully malevolent poise.

But the static did not follow him.

No jaunty 1920s music hummed beneath his presence. No warped laughter crackled from nowhere and everywhere at once. No voice— Silky, grand, brimming with mockery and menace— Filled the space.

Alastor opened his mouth.

Nothing.

The smile did not falter, but something colder flickered behind his red eyes. He tried again, drawing in a breath out of sheer habit. He FELT the words form in his mind, felt the instinct to project them outwards, amplified and distorted as they always were.

Still nothing.

No sound. Not even a whisper.

For the first time since his arrival in Hell, Alastor was truly, utterly silent.

He snapped his fingers sharply in front of his face.

The sound echoed— Too loud in the empty lobby—and he stiffened. The snap was normal. Solid. Real. His hearing worked just fine. The problem was unmistakably, infuriatingly his voice.

Slowly, Alastor raised one clawed hand to his throat. He pressed two fingers against it, as though expecting to feel some phantom wound, some cruel joke etched into his flesh. There was nothing. No pain. No damage. His shadow, stretched long across the carpet, mirrored the motion obediently… Though, it twitched a half-second too late, as if confused.

That, too, was wrong.

Alastor’s eyes narrowed. His shadow had always been an extension of himself— Playful, vicious, loyal. Now it rippled uneasily, its grin wavering.

He attempted a laugh. His chest rose. His shoulders shook slightly.

Silence answered.

The Radio Demon straightened at once, the tension snapping through him like a pulled wire. His fingers curled into his palms. Magic stirred instinctively beneath his skin, ancient and hungry, responding to his irritation.

Normally, the air would warp. The lights would flicker. That familiar radio distortion would scream its presence into reality.

Instead, the magic sputtered.

The chandeliers trembled, then steadied. The shadows along the walls twitched but did not obey.

Alastor’s smile finally strained— Not fading, it never faded, but sharpening into something brittle.

This was not a prank. Not a clever curse he could laugh off on air. Something fundamental had been taken from him, something deeply intertwined with his power and identity.

His voice was not merely how he spoke. It was how he ruled.

Footsteps approached from down the hall. “Uh… Alastor?” Charlie peeked around the corner, clipboard hugged to her chest. Her smile was as bright as ever, but uncertainty crept into it when she took in the scene. The lobby was intact. Alastor was standing there, looking exactly as he always did.

Except for the silence. The absence of his static.

“Are you okay?” she asked, glancing around.

Alastor turned to face her. He inclined his head politely, the picture of old-fashioned courtesy. His mouth opened in greeting.

Nothing came out.

Charlie blinked. “…Alastor?”

He tried again, gesturing broadly now, one hand sweeping out as if introducing a grand show. His expression was animated, eyebrows arched, grin unwavering.

Still nothing.

The silence stretched.

Charlie’s smile faltered. “Is this, um… Is this a bit?”

Alastor’s eye twitched. He shook his head once, sharply.

Recognition dawned slowly on Charlie’s face, horror creeping in around the edges. “Wait. Wait, wait— Are you serious?”

Alastor folded his arms and tapped one claw against his sleeve impatiently. He nodded.

“Oh my gosh,” Charlie whispered. “You can’t talk.”

Alastor leaned forward slightly, eyes narrowing in warning, as if daring her to phrase it that way.

She clapped a hand over her mouth. “I mean—! I’m sorry! I just—Alastor, your voice is, like, your thing.”

Alastor’s grin widened in something dangerously close to a snarl. ‘Yes. Exactly.’ His shadow rippled violently across the floor.

Whatever had stolen his voice had not merely silenced him.

It had declared war.

Charlie moved closer, her concern quickly overriding her initial shock. “Okay, okay, let’s not panic,” she said, mostly to herself. “People lose their voices all the time! It could be, um… A cold?”

Alastor’s eyes flicked to her, unimpressed. He raised one brow skeptically and tilted his head, as if inviting her to continue this fascinating theory.

“Or… Laryngitis?” Charlie offered weakly.

He shrugged one shoulder, an exaggerated, theatrical motion that ended with him spreading his hands as if to say ‘Why not?’ His shadow mimicked him, though it lagged again, its movements slightly warped, like a bad broadcast signal.

Charlie noticed. Her brows knit together. “That’s… Weird,” she murmured, eyes flicking between him and the shadow. “Your shadow’s acting funny.”

Alastor followed her gaze, jaw tightening. He straightened his coat and gestured decisively to one of the lounge chairs, then pointed to himself, then made a vague swirling motion near his throat. He finished by pointing at Charlie.

“Oh! Right. Sit down. Checkup,” she said, nodding quickly. “Yeah, yeah, good idea.”

He sat with stiff elegance, crossing one leg over the other, hands folded neatly in his lap. If not for the circumstances, he might have looked perfectly at ease. But, the tension beneath his stillness was palpable, like a coiled wire.

Charlie hesitated, then reached into her pocket and pulled out a small, heart-shaped thermometer, pink, plastic, and wildly out of place against Alastor’s red pinstripes and sharp edges.

“Open up,” she said gently.

Alastor stared at the thermometer. Then at her. Slowly, he opened his mouth.

Charlie checked his temperature, humming quietly to fill the silence. The device beeped after a moment. She glanced at it. “…Huh.” She frowned. “Totally normal.”

She tried again, just in case. Same result.

“No fever,” she said, more to reassure herself than him. “That’s good! That means you’re probably not sick.”

Alastor’s eyes narrowed slightly, as if that was NOT the comforting conclusion she seemed to think it was.

Charlie set the thermometer aside and leaned in closer, peering at his face. “Any soreness? Scratchy throat? Dizziness? Chills? Weird rashes?”

He shook his head.

“You just… Can’t speak,” Charlie summarized quietly.

Alastor nodded.

Her expression shifted. The bright optimism dimmed, replaced by something more focused, more serious. She straightened, folding her hands together.

“And your magic,” she said slowly. “It’s off. I can see it. It’s like when the power flickers right before a blackout.”

Alastor nodded.

Charlie swallowed. “Okay. That’s… Not a cold.”She paced a few steps.“If you were sick, you’d feel something. And demons don’t just… Lose their voices overnight. Especially not you.” She stopped pacing and turned to face him. “Your voice isn’t just sound. It’s bound to your magic. Your contracts. Your power.”

Alastor watched her closely, his gaze calculating. Her deduction pleased him— If anything could, under the circumstances.

Charlie’s hands curled into fists at her sides. “Which means…” She hesitated, then said it. “Someone did this to you.”

The lobby seemed to darken at once, shadows stretching just a bit too far.

Alastor rose smoothly to his feet. He did not need words to convey his agreement. His eyes glinted with something sharp and dangerous, something old.

Charlie took a step back without realizing it. “Oh. Oh no. I don’t like that look.”

He lifted one finger, as if lecturing an invisible audience, then traced a slow circle in the air. His shadow followed more closely this time, writhing with agitation.

“You think it was a curse,” Charlie said. “Or some kind of spell. Something targeted.”

Alastor inclined his head.

“Someone powerful enough to mess with YOU,” she continued, dread pooling in her stomach. “And smart enough to go straight for your voice.”

His grin widened.

Charlie hugged herself. “That’s… terrifying.”

She looked up at him again, resolve hardening behind her fear. “But you’re not dealing with it alone. Okay? This is my hotel. And you’re—” she hesitated, then smiled softly, “—You’re part of it.”

Alastor paused. For a fraction of a second, something unreadable crossed his face. Then he bowed deeply, one hand over his chest, the gesture elegant and sincere. His shadow echoed it perfectly this time.

Alastor straightened. Whoever had done this had made a grave mistake.

Charlie hesitated, her brows knitting together as a familiar, uncomfortable thought surfaced. “…There IS one person I should probably ask,” she said slowly.

Alastor, who had been adjusting his gloves with meticulous care, paused. His eyes slid back to her, gleaming with mild curiosity— And a touch of suspicion.

“My dad.”

The temperature in the lobby seemed to drop a degree. Alastor’s smile sharpened at once, teeth glinting. He straightened, posture immaculate, and placed his hands behind his back. His shadow stretched tall and thin, antlers curling higher than usual.

Charlie rushed on. “I mean—I don’t think he did it. But you two have… A history. And if someone DID do this as a joke—”

Alastor let out a silent laugh, shoulders shaking once. The absence of sound made it far more unsettling than any audible cackle. He pantomimed exaggerated applause, slow and mocking, then tipped an imaginary hat.

‘Yes. Of course,’ the gesture said. ‘Lucifer Morningstar. He must be pulling a practical joke.’

Charlie sighed. “I need to check. If he didn’t do it, then we know this is serious. Like… Really serious.”

Alastor studied her for a moment, then gave a small, resigned shrug.

“Okay,” Charlie said, inhaling deeply. “Let’s go.”

Golden light poured in from impossible angles, reflecting off polished marble and gilded décor. Massive windows framed a sky of swirling fire and crimson clouds. At the center of it all, lounging across his throne like a bored housecat, was Lucifer Morningstar himself— Dapper suit, cane twirling lazily between his fingers, wings half-spread.

“Charlie!” he exclaimed brightly as she approached. “To what do I owe the pleasure? Is the hotel on fire again, or is this a social visit?”

Charlie forced a smile. “Hi, Dad. Uh… Kind of a medical emergency? But not mine.”

Alastor entered the throne room without hesitation, cane in hand, grin sharp and unyielding. Even silent, his presence cut through the space like a blade. The radio static that should have accompanied him did not, and Lucifer noticed that immediately.

The Devil’s smile twitched. “Well I’ll be damned,” Lucifer said, straightening. “You’re actually quiet.”

Alastor bowed with exaggerated politeness, flourishing his cane. His shadow bowed too, though it lingered a beat longer, looming.

Charlie clasped her hands together. “Dad… Did you do something to Alastor?”

Lucifer blinked. Once. Then he laughed. “Oh, that’s rich.”

Charlie didn’t laugh with him. “I’m serious.”

Lucifer’s laughter faded. He looked back at Alastor, eyes narrowing with sharp, ancient perception. “You ARE serious,” he said slowly. “

Alastor tilted his head slightly, smile fixed.

Alastor’s eye twitched.

Charlie frowned. “Dad.”

He leaned in closer to Alastor, their grins mirroring each other— One smug, one razor-sharp.

“And frankly,” Lucifer added, “if I ever took your voice, you’d KNOW it was me.”

Alastor inclined his head, conceding the point.

Charlie exhaled. “So you didn’t do it.”

Lucifer straightened, expression turning uncharacteristically serious. “No. And I don’t like that someone else could.”

He tapped the floor with his cane, sending a ripple of power through the room. The air hummed in response, but when it brushed against Alastor, it slid off him— Deflected, distorted.

Lucifer frowned. “Interesting. Whatever this is, it’s anchored deep. Bound to his essence, not his body.”

Charlie hugged herself. “Can you fix it?”

Lucifer looked at Alastor again, eyes sharp with reluctant respect. “If I could, I would’ve already. This isn’t brute force magic. It’s… Targeted. Personal.”

Alastor’s shadow curled inwards slightly, bristling.

Lucifer sighed. “Which means someone who knows you. Your power. Your weaknesses.”

Charlie’s heart sank.

Lucifer turned to her. “You were right to bring him to me. But if you want answers? You’ll have to look elsewhere.”

As Alastor turned to leave, Lucifer called after him. “For what it’s worth, Radio Demon—if I find out who did this?” Lucifer’s eyes glowed faintly gold. “I’ll let you have first crack at them.”

Alastor paused at the doorway. He nodded in acknowledgment.
