“Then prove it.”

That did it.

The lights shattered overhead, plunging the room into flickering darkness as Alastor’s power surged uncontrolled for a split second. The camera sparked, smoke curling from it as the feed cut out with a sharp electronic whine.

Vox laughed anyway.

“Oooh, struck a nerve!” he said, voice still ringing through hidden speakers. “Careful, Alastor. Getting worked up won’t help.”

Alastor braced himself against the wall now, abandoning pretense. His smile was rawer, more desperate. The pressure had become unbearable, his body caught in a cruel stalemate between need and panic. Every second Vox lingered outside made it worse.

“Leave,” Alastor growled, the word vibrating with barely restrained fury. “Now.”

There was a pause.

For a moment, Vox said nothing.

Then… Footsteps.

Slow. Deliberate. Moving AWAY.

“Another time, then,” Vox said lightly. “Wouldn’t want you… Hurting yourself.”

True silence followed— Heavy, blessed, and absolute.

Alastor sagged, breathing hard, radio static fading into a low, uneven hum. The pain didn’t vanish immediately, but without the weight of Vox’s attention pressing down on him, the tension began— Slowly, mercifully— To ease.

He closed his eyes, forcing himself to breathe. “I will end him,” he muttered weakly.

But for now… He just needed the world to stop watching.

The relief was supposed to come with the silence.

It didn’t.

Alastor remained full, breathing measured, eyes closed as he tried to coax his body into cooperation. The pressure hadn’t eased much— If anything, the prolonged tension had begun to spread, radiating outwards into his lower back and sides, a deep, uncomfortable ache that made it difficult to stand perfectly straight.

He rolled one shoulder, then the other, trying to loosen the stiffness creeping up his spine. His coat felt heavier than usual. Every movement reminded him how long he’d been standing there, rigid, fighting himself as much as the situation.

“Focus,” he murmured, voice low and strained. “You’ve endured worse than this.”

His muscles disagreed.

A sharp twinge flared along his side as he shifted his weight, drawing a quiet hiss through clenched teeth. The effort of holding still— Of pretending he had control— Was starting to hurt. Not dramatically. Just persistently. The kind of pain that wore a person down.

Then—

The restroom door opened again.

Alastor’s eyes flew open. ‘FUCK! Why didn’t I lock it?!’

“Oh, don’t tell me you thought I was done,” Vox said cheerfully, his voice bouncing off the tile like a bad echo. “You sounded SO tense when I left. I figured I should check in.”

Alastor straightened instantly, posture snapping back into place through sheer force of will. The movement sent another dull ache through his back, but he ignored it, turning slowly towards the sound.

“You are testing your lifespan,” he said evenly.

Vox’s screen-face was glowing with amusement. He leaned against the counter, entirely too comfortable.

“Relax,” Vox said.

Alastor’s static crackled, sharp and erratic. “You have made that impossible.”

Vox laughed. “You know, it’s funny. You’ve always been one of the most powerful demons in Hell, and the whole time, all it took was me standing here to completely break you.”

Alastor’s jaw tightened. The ache along his sides deepened as his muscles involuntarily tensed again, the stress undoing what little progress he’d managed. He pressed a hand briefly to his lower back, then stopped, forcing himself still. “Leave,” he repeated, quieter now, but no less dangerous.

Vox’s grin widened. “What’s the rush? You look uncomfortable.” He tapped his chin. “Back hurting? Sides all tight? I did a little research on your piss thing… That’s supposed to happen when you can’t go for too long.”

The accuracy of it made Alastor’s stomach twist. He drew in a slow breath, but it caught halfway, his body protesting the continued strain. His reflection in the mirror looked agonized now— Smile quavering, eyes sharp with frustration and severe desperation.

“You enjoy this,” Alastor said flatly.

“Oh, absolutely,” Vox replied without shame. “Seeing you lose control? Worth every second… Let’s see what happens when I do this!” He placed his hand flat against Alastor’s back, right below his ribs, and then he pressed down.

“Ah—Ah!” Radio static screeched, punctuating Alastor’s yelps. An enormous pulse of pain flared, sharper than ever, forcing Alastor to brace himself against the wall again. Vox’s hand was like a chainsaw ripping deep into his core and tearing him into shreds. He closed his eyes, not in defeat, but to keep from lashing out blindly. The effort it took to remain still, silent, contained was immense.

“Awww! Poor Alastor!” Vox sarcastically cooed. “Does that hurt?” He pressed harder and harder, making Alastor feel like he was going to rip in half. “Maybe I can finally get that fucking grin off your face!”

Alastor forced himself to smile even harder. His teeth ached from how tightly they were clenched. “You are playing a very dangerous game,” he said, voice wavering.

“YOU are my prisoner.” Vox leaned closer, lowering his voice. “You do as I say.”

Alastor’s control finally began to fracture.

The ache in his back and sides had grown into something far worse— An all-consuming pressure that made it hard to breathe steadily, let alone stand. Every muscle felt locked in place, pulled tight by stress and refusal and the relentless passage of time. It hurt to move. It hurt to stay still. There was no position that eased it.

Static sputtered faintly in the air like a damaged broadcast signal. “Enough,” Alastor whispered— To his body, to the situation, to himself.

But nothing changed.

He couldn’t go.

It was all he wanted to do, and it was impossible to do it.

A sharp spike of pain shot through his side as he shifted, sudden and vicious enough that his breath broke completely. He sucked in air, shoulders trembling despite his efforts to remain composed. His vision blurred at the edges, the fluorescent lights smearing into dull halos. And then— Traitorously, unforgivably— His eyes burned.

A tear slipped free before he could stop it, gliding silently down his cheek.

Alastor froze in horror.

“No,” he hissed, voice cracking with distorted static. His gloved hand flew up, wiping at his face as if he could erase the evidence. Another tear followed anyway, then another, and another, more and more— Small, quiet, entirely out of his control. His breath hitched with what could only be called a SOB.

The shame hit harder than the pain.

He was… No, he couldn’t be… He couldn’t… He hadn’t even cried when Adam injured him, and that had been angelic steel… But, this hurt just as bad, and there wasn’t even anything valiant in it to soften the blow…

This was weakness. This was exposure. This was everything he despised in himself, laid bare while his oldest rival looked on. He turned his face away sharply, teeth clenched so hard they ached.“I will not—” His voice broke, and he stopped, swallowing hard.

Vox froze.

The electronic hum of his screen softened, the sharpness in his posture easing as he actually LOOKED at Alastor— Not the towering Radio Demon, not the grin and bravado, but the figure hunched slightly against the wall, shaking despite himself.

“…Hey,” Vox said, quieter. “Al… Are you crying?”

Alastor stiffened. “Ha…” he huffed out weakly. “No.”

Vox took an uncertain step closer, the teasing tone gone entirely. “I didn’t think—” He stopped, screen flickering faintly. “I didn’t know it was this bad.”

Alastor laughed once, harsh and broken. “How entertaining for you,” he spat, though there was no venom left in it. “You got your show.”

Vox shook his head. “No. This isn’t—” He hesitated, old memories surfacing unbidden: Late nights drinking together, laughter crackling through shared static, a time before rejection and rivalry calcified into cruelty. “This isn’t what I wanted.”

Alastor wiped his face again, mortified. His shoulders were trembling openly now, the pain and humiliation blending into something unbearable. “I am useless,” he muttered.

“Don’t say that,” Vox said quickly, the words surprising even himself. “You’re not.”

Alastor turned on him sharply, eyes blazing wetly. “You told me to say it!”

Vox didn’t look away. “You’re hurting,” he said simply. “And I pushed you there.”

Silence fell— Thick, heavy, unfamiliar.

Vox exhaled. “I thought it’d just be funny. Rattle you a bit.” His voice dropped. “I forgot you’re not… Indestructible.”

Alastor looked away again, jaw trembling. “Leave,” he said weakly. “Please.”

The word please hung in the air like a broken signal.

Vox’s screen dimmed slightly. “…Okay,” he said. “I will.” He hesitated at the door, then added, softer, “I’m sorry, Al.”

The door closed.

This time, the silence stayed.

Alastor sagged fully against the wall, breath shuddering as he covered his face with one hand, radio static fading into a low, wounded hum. The pain was still there— Sharp, unrelenting, torturous.

He hated that Vox had seen him like this.

He hated even more that Vox had CARED.

The silence stretched on, heavy but no longer hostile.

Alastor remained where he was, pressed against the stall wall, breathing shallowly as the last tremors worked their way out of his shoulders. His face felt hot with shame; The echo of his own tears lingered in his mind like feedback he couldn’t quite mute.

He didn’t notice Vox was still out in the hall until a sound filled the room. Soft. Steady. Water.

Not loud, not intrusive— Just the gentle, continuous rush of it, as if a sink somewhere nearby had been turned on and forgotten about. It reverberated faintly through the tiles.

The sound continued, consistent and grounding. His body reacted. The relentless tension he’d been holding began to waver, then loosen, like a knot finally giving way after being pulled too tight for too long.

His breath hitched.

The pain that had been clawing at his back and sides began, mercifully, to ebb as a stream of liquid relief gushed out of him. The ache dulled, then softened into something manageable. His shoulders sagged as the strain drained away, leaving behind a deep, almost dizzying sense of bliss that made his knees feel weak.

Alastor braced himself, eyes squeezed shut, static crackling unevenly around him as his body finally— FINALLY—cooperated.

He exhaled a long, trembling breath. “Ahhhhhh…”

For a moment, he felt nothing but relief. Pure and overwhelming. Like coming up for air after being held underwater far too long. His muscles slackened, the sharp edge of pain dissolving into exhaustion.

As soon as he was empty, the shame returned.

Vox had seen him break. Cry. Beg.

The water sound faded, clicking off softly.

“…You okay?” Vox asked, hesitant.

Alastor didn’t answer right away. When he did, his voice was quiet, stripped of bravado and radio-show polish. “I will never forgive myself for this,” he said flatly.

Vox winced. “Hey. Don’t—”

“You witnessed weakness,” Alastor continued, not looking at him. “I will carry that humiliation until the end of time.”

“That’s not what I saw,” Vox said.

Alastor laughed bitterly. “Then you are lying to spare my pride.”

Vox shook his head. “I saw someone who needed help. And let me help.” A pause. “Like before. All the times I helped you walk home.”

“Do not romanticize this,” Alastor warned weakly.

“I’m not,” Vox replied. “I’m owning my part in it.”

Silence settled again.

Alastor straightened slowly, forcing his posture back into something dignified, even as exhaustion weighed heavy in his limbs. He left the restroom, returned to his chair, said nothing as Vox tied him back up.

Vox hesitated before leaving. “For what it’s worth… I’m sorry I hurt you.”

“Bullshit,” Alastor said. “You’re gonna broadcast this. Tell all of Hell that you THINK you saw me cry.”

“Huh?!” Vox actually had the audacity to look surprised. “No, I wouldn’t—“

“You loved every second of that!” Alastor said. “It got you off!”

“I was just fucking with you! I—“

“Just go! Do whatever you’re going to do!”

The door closed.

Alastor remained alone, breathing steady now, relief still humming faintly through his body.

He would survive the shame.

He always did.

Somehow.

***

Vox didn’t go far.

He told himself he would— Took three steps down the hall, then five more— But, his feet slowed anyway, shoes scuffing against the polished floor.

Vox leaned against the wall, folding his arms as his screen dimmed to a low, restless glow. The usual ads and idle visuals refused to surface. All he saw instead was the image burned into his processors: Alastor hunched, shaking, eyes bright with tears he’d tried— And failed— To hide.

Tears Vox had caused.

“Idiot,” Vox muttered, rubbing a hand over his face. “Real classy.”

It hadn’t started that way. It never did. Fucking with Alastor was habit. Muscle memory from a rivalry that had built over decades. Push, prod, needle until the Radio Demon snapped back with that sharp grin and sharper threats. It was their dance. Their thing.

Except this time, Alastor hadn’t snapped. He’d folded.

And Vox hadn’t stopped.

The guilt hit late but hard, spreading through him like corrupted code. He replayed every word he’d said, every smug comment, every second he’d stayed when he should’ve left. He’d thought he was poking at pride.

He’d been digging into something fragile.

“I used to know you,” Vox murmured to the empty hallway.

Once, long ago, he HAD known Alastor. Known the cadence of his laughter when it wasn’t performative. Known the way his static softened late at night when the world finally stopped listening. Known the strange comfort of sharing silence without an audience.

Vox had loved him.

Deeply enough that it still lingered, dormant and inconvenient, surfacing at the worst possible moments— Like now, when he realized he’d just tormented someone he used to cherish.

Vox clenched his fists.

“What the hell happened to us?” he whispered.

Power, probably. Pride. The need to be louder, brighter, better. Somewhere along the way, affection had curdled into competition, then cruelty. And Vox had told himself Alastor was untouchable, too sharp, too strong to ever really be hurt.

Seeing him cry shattered that lie completely.

Vox swallowed, screen flickering with low static of his own. “I’m sorry,” he said again, even though Alastor couldn’t hear it now, and wouldn’t have believed him anyway. “I really am.”

He knew better than to try to fix this. Alastor would hate that. Hate the implication that he needed tending to, that Vox’s guilt mattered more than his dignity.

But Vox also knew this wouldn’t leave him.

The image. The shame. The memory of choosing to hurt someone he still, stupidly, cared about.

He pushed off the wall at last, straightening, expression hardening back into something passable. The Vees couldn’t see cracks. Couldn’t see regret.

Still, as he walked away, Vox cast one last glance over his shoulder towards the closed door.

Old feelings, once awakened, were hard to silence.
